=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and an integer k.
You are allowed to perform the following operation on each element of the array at most once:

Add an integer in the range [-k, k] to the element.

Return the maximum possible number of distinct elements in nums after performing the operations.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,2,3,3,4], k = 2
Output: 6
Explanation:
nums changes to [-1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4] after performing operations on the first four elements.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [4,4,4,4], k = 1
Output: 3
Explanation:
By adding -1 to nums[0] and 1 to nums[1], nums changes to [3, 5, 4, 4].

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^9
0 <= k <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxDistinctElements(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxDistinctElements(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        ans = 0
        last = -10**30  # or float('-inf') but integer compare with float okay; use large negative
        for x in nums:
            left = x - k
            right = x + k
            val = max(left, last + 1)
            if val <= right:
                ans += 1
                last = val
        return ans
```

class Solution:
    def maxDistinctElements(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxDistinctElements(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        ans = 0
        last = -10**30
        for x in nums:
            left = x - k
            right = x + k
            val = max(left, last + 1)
            if val <= right:
                ans += 1
                last = val
        return ans
```
This is concise.

Let's ensure code block no extra text. The final answer should be:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxDistinctElements(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        ans = 0
        last = -10**30
        for x in nums:
            left = x - k
            right = x + k
            val = max(left, last + 1)
            if val <= right:
                ans += 1
                last = val
        return ans
```

Let's maybe attempt to prove the greedy with `last` in a more formal way for my confidence:
Let intervals I_i = [a_i,b_i] with a_i,b_i nondecreasing (since a_i = nums_i-k, b_i=nums_i+k). We process in this order. Maintain `last` = largest value assigned so far. Claim: after processing i, for every unprocessed interval j>i, if a_j <= last, then every integer in [a_j, last] has already been assigned. This is the key. We proved by induction. Then when processing interval i, if a_i <= last, all integers in [a_i,last] are assigned, so the smallest unassigned integer >= a_i is last+1. If a_i > last, no assigned integer >= a_i (since last is largest), so smallest unassigned is a_i. Thus val = max(a_i,last+1) is the smallest available point >= a_i. If val <= b_i, assign; otherwise no available point in interval. This is exactly the standard greedy. Since standard greedy is optimal, ans is max matching. Good.

Let's ensure the induction invariant's base and step with skip:
Base before processing first: last=-inf, no unprocessed? For all j, a_j <= -inf false, invariant true.
Step:
- If assign val:
  - If val=a_i (a_i>last): last'=a_i. For j>i, a_j >= a_i = last'. If a_j <= last', then a_j=last'. Need [last',last'] assigned; yes val=a_i. If a_j>last', condition false. Invariant holds.
  - If val=last+1 (a_i<=last): last'=last+1. For j>i:
    * If a_j <= last: by previous invariant for interval i? Wait previous invariant before processing i says for all unprocessed intervals j>=i, if a_j <= last then [a_j,last] assigned. This includes j>i. So [a_j,last] assigned. val=last+1 assigned, so [a_j,last'] assigned.
    * If a_j = last+1: [last+1,last+1] assigned.
    * If a_j > last+1: condition false.
    * If a_j between? Since integer, if a_j <= last or =last+1 or >last+1. Good.
- If skip: no assignment, last unchanged. Previous invariant for j>i remains true. But wait previous invariant before processing i was for all unprocessed intervals j>=i. It included current i. For j>i, it remains. Good.
Thus invariant holds.

Now, standard greedy optimal proof:
For intervals sorted by right endpoint, let I be first interval. Let q be smallest available point >= a_i chosen by greedy (or skip if none). Consider an optimal matching M. The standard greedy proof for interval scheduling with points usually sorts intervals by right endpoint and assigns smallest available point; optimality can be shown by induction using earliest finish time and exchange with the interval matched to the chosen point? Let's recall.

Actually the standard greedy for "maximum number of intervals matched to distinct points" when points are given and intervals sorted by right endpoint: For each interval, assign the smallest available point >= left. This is optimal. Proof: Let I be first interval, let p be the smallest available point >= left(I). If p exists. Consider an optimal solution. If I is not matched, add I->p? p might be used by another interval in optimal. But if p is used by another interval J, since J's right >= I's right and p <= I's right, p is also in I. We can swap: assign I to p, and assign J to the point that I was assigned in optimal (if I was assigned) or leave J unmatched? Wait if I was unmatched in optimal and p used by J, then we can't simply add. But maybe we can match I to p and unmatch J, size same. If I was unmatched, optimal size M, adding I would increase unless p used. If p used by J, unmatch J and match I, size same. So there is an optimal with I matched to p. If I was matched to some p' in optimal, p' >= p? Since p is smallest available >= left, but p' may be less than p? p' is used in optimal, but p is available in greedy sense (not used by earlier intervals, but could be used in optimal). If p' < p, then p' >= left and p' is available? It is used by I in optimal, so not available. Greedy chose p because all points in [left, p-1] are used by earlier intervals? Wait before processing first interval, no earlier intervals, so p = left(I). Thus p is the left endpoint. If I matched to p' > p, can replace with p because p is not used by any interval in optimal? Is p maybe used by another interval? Since p = left(I), and I has earliest right. Another interval J using p has right >= right(I) and left <= p. Could be. If p used by J, and I matched to p', can we swap I to p and J to p'? Need p' <= right(I) <= right(J), and p' >= left(I) = p >= left(J)? Since J uses p, left(J) <= p. p' >= p >= left(J). So p' is in J's interval. Thus swap works. If p not used, just replace. So there is optimal with I matched to p. Then remove I and p and recurse. This proof works for first interval because p = left(I) (smallest available >= left is left, since no earlier assigned). Wait after removing earlier intervals, p may not be left(I) because earlier intervals used some points < left? Actually earlier intervals are removed, and their assigned points are no longer available? In the recursive subproblem, the remaining available points exclude points used by earlier greedy choices. For the current first remaining interval, p = smallest available >= left. It may be > left because points between left and p-1 were used by earlier intervals. But those earlier intervals are fixed and removed; in the subproblem, p is the smallest available. The exchange proof can be adapted: Let I be current first interval, p chosen. Consider optimal solution for subproblem (remaining intervals and remaining points). If I unmatched, if p unused, add; if p used by J, swap? If I unmatched and p used by J, match I to p and unmatch J, size same. So there is optimal with I matched to p. If I matched to p' in optimal, p' >= p? Wait in the subproblem, available points exclude earlier used points. An optimal matching for remaining intervals can only use available points. So p' is available. Thus p' >= p. Good. If p' > p, and p is available but maybe used by another interval J in optimal. If p used by J, swap I to p and J to p'. Need p' in J's interval: p' <= right(I) <= right(J), and p' >= p >= left(J) (since J uses p, left(J) <= p). So yes. Thus optimal with I matched to p. Then recurse. This proves greedy.
